[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I’ve just stopped caring about everything I used to actually look forward to. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the part of me that used to get interested in things just shut off.\n\nI’ve got fishing gear sitting out in the garage, and normally I’d be checking it, organizing tackle, thinking about when I could get out on the water. Now it’s just sitting there untouched. Same with my woodshop — I used to lose hours in there, making little projects, fixing things, just working with my hands. Lately I walk in and it feels like another chore I don’t want to deal with.\n\nEven with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess, and I’ll sit there because I don’t want to disappoint them, but I’m not really in it. I’m just moving pieces around, going through the motions. And that’s not like me. That’s really why I’m here, I think. I don’t feel connected to any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to, or has the pleasure gone away too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the pleasure’s pretty much gone. I mean, I keep trying to tell myself maybe I’m just tired or busy, but it’s more than that. These are things I used to actually look forward to.\n\nMy fishing gear is still sitting out in the garage untouched. Normally I’d be messing with it, checking lines, sorting tackle, thinking about where I might go next, even if I didn’t have time to get out right away. Now I see it there and just… nothing. It doesn’t pull me in at all.\n\nSame with my woodshop. I used to love being in there, just making something or fixing something with my hands. I could lose track of time. Lately I walk in and it feels like a chore before I’ve even started, like it’s another responsibility instead of something I enjoy.\n\nAnd with my kids, that’s probably the part that bothers me most. They’ll ask me to play chess, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want them to feel brushed off, but I’m not really there. I’m moving the pieces, saying the right things, but inside it’s flat. I’m just going through the motions. So yeah… it’s not just that I’m doing less. It’s that even when I do the things, I don’t feel much from them anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. About a year and seven months, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt really started after my wife’s health problems changed everything at home. I don’t think it happened all at once, like one day I woke up and everything was flat. It was more like things kept shifting, and I kept telling myself I just had to get through the next week, then the next thing, then the next appointment or adjustment at home… and somewhere in there I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nAt first I thought it was just stress, or that I was worn out from everything changing. But it’s been over a year and a half now, and it hasn’t really lifted. That’s part of what scares me, honestly — that it’s gone on this long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there making some detailed plan all day or anything, but there are these moments that scare me. Usually it’s early in the morning, when I’m driving before anyone else is really awake, and everything’s quiet. I’ll be on the road and this thought just comes in like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t make it where I’m going.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further, where I catch myself thinking about steering off the road. That’s the part that really scares me, because it doesn’t feel as distant as I want it to. It feels too close, like it’s not just some random dark thought I can brush away. I don’t want to talk about it, but I guess I’m here because it’s been happening and it’s frightening me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of restlessness, nervousness, or anxiety lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m pacing around all the time or anything like that, at least not that I’ve really noticed. Nervousness… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if I’d call it anxiety or just being wound up from everything. It’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. A lot of the time I just feel heavy and worn down, and then other times there’s this tense feeling in the background, like I can’t quite settle, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t been paying attention to it as its own thing. I notice more that I’m not myself, but if you’re asking specifically whether I feel restless or anxious, I’d say… maybe some, but I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I haven’t really been weighing myself every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I can tell from my clothes.\n\nMy belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t do anything to make that happen. I’m not dieting, I’m not trying to get in shape. If anything, with work, I should probably be eating more, especially being out on job sites all day. But most days food just… doesn’t appeal to me. It sounds strange, but it’s like everything tastes like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast without really deciding to, just because nothing sounds worth eating, and then by dinner I’ll sit there and kind of pick at the food.\n\nSometimes I know I should be hungry, because I’ve been moving around or dealing with a long day, but I just don’t want it. So, yeah… I guess I have lost weight, even if I don’t know the exact amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself speaking slower, exactly… but I’m probably not the best judge of that. I mean, I know sometimes I take longer to answer people, but that might just be because I’m trying to think of what to say or I don’t have much to say.\n\nWith moving slower… maybe? I don’t know if it’s actually slower or if everything just feels like it takes more effort. Like getting up, getting started, doing the next thing — it can feel like I’m dragging myself through it. But I haven’t had anyone say, “Why are you moving so slow?” or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed it as moving or talking slower, not in a way I can point to. It’s more that I feel slowed down inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know how much that shows on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends or is that also something that doesn't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… friends too, I guess. It’s not just friends, though. It’s kind of everything I used to actually look forward to.\n\nLike, I used to be the guy who’d be thinking about fishing even when I was at work—checking gear, planning where I might go, messing with the tackle box in the garage. And now all that stuff is just sitting there. I see it when I walk past and I just… don’t care. It’s not that I decided I don’t like fishing anymore. It’s more like the part of me that would get excited about it just isn’t there.\n\nSame with my woodshop. I used to be able to spend hours out there and lose track of time. Now if I go in there, it feels like another task I’m supposed to do, like a chore, not something I enjoy. I’ll look at a project and just feel tired of it before I even start.\n\nAnd with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess sometimes, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to let them down, but I’m not really into it. I’m moving the pieces and trying to act normal, but inside I’m just kind of going through the motions. So with friends, yeah, I’ll avoid plans or I’ll show up and not really feel present. It doesn’t bring me much pleasure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I’m not, like, pacing around the room or tapping my foot so much people notice, at least I don’t think I am.\n\nI mean, sometimes I shift around if I’m sitting too long, but I’ve always done some of that. On job sites I’m used to being up and moving, so sitting still in a chair can feel weird after a while, but I wouldn’t say I feel keyed up or like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit there longer than I mean to sometimes.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed fidgeting or being unable to sit still as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly. It’s like everything gets turned back on me somehow.\n\nI keep thinking I’ve failed my wife and my kids. Like I’m supposed to be the steady one, the guy who handles things, and instead I feel like I’m letting them down all the time. Even little things — if I miss an appointment, or forget something I said I’d take care of, it doesn’t just feel like a mistake. It feels like proof that I’m not any good at this, at being a husband or a father.\n\nAnd work is the same way. I manage crews, so there are always things going wrong — costs running over, schedules slipping, some safety issue that needs attention — and I know, logically, some of that is just the job. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head. It feels like if anything goes wrong, it’s because I missed something, or I should’ve been sharper, or I shouldn’t be the one in charge at all. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking, “How did I let that happen?” Even if other people tell me it wasn’t all on me, I don’t really believe them.\n\nIt gets to where I feel useless. Like everyone would be better off if someone else was handling things instead of me. At home, at work… just anywhere I’m supposed to be responsible. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to initiate activities as easily as you used to?\n\nPatient: No… not like I used to. Nervousness… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if I’d call it anxiety or just being wound up from everything. It’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. A lot of the time I just feel heavy and worn down, and then other times there’s this tense feeling in the background, like I can’t quite settle, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t been paying attention to it as its own thing. I notice more that I’m not myself, but if you’re asking specifically whether I feel restless or anxious, I’d say… maybe some, but I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I haven’t really been weighing myself every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I can tell from my clothes.\n\nMy belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t do anything to make that happen. I’m not dieting, I’m not trying to get in shape. If anything, with work, I should probably be eating more, especially being out on job sites all day. But most days food just… doesn’t appeal to me. It sounds strange, but it’s like everything tastes like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast without really deciding to, just because nothing sounds worth eating, and then by dinner I’ll sit there and kind of pick at the food.\n\nSometimes I know I should be hungry, because I’ve been moving around or dealing with a long day, but I just don’t want it. So, yeah… I guess I have lost weight, even if I don’t know the exact amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself speaking slower, exactly… but I’m probably not the best judge of that. I mean, I know sometimes I take longer to answer people, but that might just be because I’m trying to think of what to say or I don’t have much to say.\n\nWith moving slower… maybe? I don’t know if it’s actually slower or if everything just feels like it takes more effort. Like getting up, getting started, doing the next thing — it can feel like I’m dragging myself through it. But I haven’t had anyone say, “Why are you moving so slow?” or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed it as moving or talking slower, not in a way I can point to. It’s more that I feel slowed down inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know how much that shows on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends or is that also something that doesn't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… friends too, I guess. It’s not just friends, though. It’s kind of everything I used to actually look forward to.\n\nLike, I used to be the guy who’d be thinking about fishing even when I was at work—checking gear, planning where I might go, messing with the tackle box in the garage. And now all that stuff is just sitting there. I see it when I walk past and I just… don’t care. It’s not that I decided I don’t like fishing anymore. It’s more like the part of me that would get excited about it just isn’t there.\n\nSame with my woodshop. I used to be able to spend hours out there and lose track of time. Now if I go in there, it feels like another task I’m supposed to do, like a chore, not something I enjoy. I’ll look at a project and just feel tired of it before I even start.\n\nAnd with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess sometimes, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to let them down, but I’m not really into it. I’m moving the pieces and trying to act normal, but inside I’m just kind of going through the motions. So with friends, yeah, I’ll avoid plans or I’ll show up and not really feel present. It doesn’t bring me much pleasure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I’m not, like, pacing around the room or tapping my foot so much people notice, at least I don’t think I am.\n\nI mean, sometimes I shift around if I’m sitting too long, but I’ve always done some of that. On job sites I’m used to being up and moving, so sitting still in a chair can feel weird after a while, but I wouldn’t say I feel keyed up or like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit there longer than I mean to sometimes.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed fidgeting or being unable to sit still as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly. It’s like everything gets turned back on me somehow.\n\nI keep thinking I’ve failed my wife and my kids. Like I’m supposed to be the steady one, the guy who handles things, and instead I feel like I’m letting them down all the time. Even little things — if I miss an appointment, or forget something I said I’d take care of, it doesn’t just feel like a mistake. It feels like proof that I’m not any good at this, at being a husband or a father.\n\nAnd work is the same way. I manage crews, so there are always things going wrong — costs running over, schedules slipping, some safety issue that needs attention — and I know, logically, some of that is just the job. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head. It feels like if anything goes wrong, it’s because I missed something, or I should’ve been sharper, or I shouldn’t be the one in charge at all. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking, “How did I let that happen?” Even if other people tell me it wasn’t all on me, I don’t really believe them.\n\nIt gets to where I feel useless. Like everyone would be better off if someone else was handling things instead of me. At home, at work… just anywhere I’m supposed to be responsible. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to initiate activities as easily as you used to?\n\nPatient: No… not like I used to. It feels like there’s this extra weight between thinking about doing something and actually doing it.\n\nI can know, in my head, “I should go out to the garage and sort my fishing gear,” or “I could go spend an hour in the woodshop,” but then I just… don’t. The fishing stuff is still sitting there untouched, and that used to be something I’d mess with just because I enjoyed it. Now even getting started feels pointless, like I’m trying to force myself into being interested.\n\nSame with the woodshop. I’ll walk out there sometimes, look around, and instead of feeling that pull to start a project, it just feels like another chore waiting on me. And with my kids, if they ask me to play chess, I’ll do it because they’re my kids and I don’t want to disappoint them, but I’m not the one initiating it. I’m mostly just sitting there going through the motions.\n\nSo, no, I’d say starting things is a lot harder now. It’s not even that the activities are complicated. I just don’t seem to have that spark to begin them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving at a slower pace than usual in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t notice myself walking slower in the way somebody would look at me and say, “What’s wrong with you?” At least nobody’s said that to me.\n\nDay to day, though… I do feel like things take longer, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m physically moving slower or because I’m just slower to get going. Like getting dressed, making coffee, getting out the door — sometimes it feels like I’m dragging through it, but I can’t tell if my body is actually slow or if it’s more that I’m kind of stuck in my head and not moving from one thing to the next.\n\nAt work I can still move around a site when I need to. If there’s something that has to be handled, I can walk over there, talk to whoever, check what needs checking. I don’t think I’m shuffling around or anything like that. But when there isn’t something pushing me, I probably do sort of… stall out. Sit longer. Take more time than I mean to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed that I’m moving slower, exactly. More that everything feels harder to start and takes more effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have been talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mean, I’m probably quieter than I used to be, or I take longer to answer sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as talking more slowly.\n\nNobody at home or work has said, “You’re speaking slower,” or anything like that. Sometimes I do feel like I have to search for the words a little more, or I pause because I’m trying to get my thoughts in order, but I can’t tell if it comes across as slow speech from the outside.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t caught myself speaking slower in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a significant weight loss, like more than 5% of your body weight, over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, or at least it seems like it. I haven’t been carefully tracking the number on the scale or anything, so I don’t want to pretend I can give you an exact percentage. I’ll be on the road and this thought just comes in like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t make it where I’m going.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further, where I catch myself thinking about steering off the road. That’s the part that really scares me, because it doesn’t feel as distant as I want it to. It feels too close, like it’s not just some random dark thought I can brush away. I don’t want to talk about it, but I guess I’m here because it’s been happening and it’s frightening me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of restlessness, nervousness, or anxiety lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m pacing around all the time or anything like that, at least not that I’ve really noticed. Nervousness… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if I’d call it anxiety or just being wound up from everything. It’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. A lot of the time I just feel heavy and worn down, and then other times there’s this tense feeling in the background, like I can’t quite settle, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t been paying attention to it as its own thing. I notice more that I’m not myself, but if you’re asking specifically whether I feel restless or anxious, I’d say… maybe some, but I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I haven’t really been weighing myself every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I can tell from my clothes.\n\nMy belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t do anything to make that happen. I’m not dieting, I’m not trying to get in shape. If anything, with work, I should probably be eating more, especially being out on job sites all day. But most days food just… doesn’t appeal to me. It sounds strange, but it’s like everything tastes like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast without really deciding to, just because nothing sounds worth eating, and then by dinner I’ll sit there and kind of pick at the food.\n\nSometimes I know I should be hungry, because I’ve been moving around or dealing with a long day, but I just don’t want it. So, yeah… I guess I have lost weight, even if I don’t know the exact amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself speaking slower, exactly… but I’m probably not the best judge of that. I mean, I know sometimes I take longer to answer people, but that might just be because I’m trying to think of what to say or I don’t have much to say.\n\nWith moving slower… maybe? I don’t know if it’s actually slower or if everything just feels like it takes more effort. Like getting up, getting started, doing the next thing — it can feel like I’m dragging myself through it. But I haven’t had anyone say, “Why are you moving so slow?” or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed it as moving or talking slower, not in a way I can point to. It’s more that I feel slowed down inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know how much that shows on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends or is that also something that doesn't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… friends too, I guess. It’s not just friends, though. It’s kind of everything I used to actually look forward to.\n\nLike, I used to be the guy who’d be thinking about fishing even when I was at work—checking gear, planning where I might go, messing with the tackle box in the garage. And now all that stuff is just sitting there. I see it when I walk past and I just… don’t care. It’s not that I decided I don’t like fishing anymore. It’s more like the part of me that would get excited about it just isn’t there.\n\nSame with my woodshop. I used to be able to spend hours out there and lose track of time. Now if I go in there, it feels like another task I’m supposed to do, like a chore, not something I enjoy. I’ll look at a project and just feel tired of it before I even start.\n\nAnd with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess sometimes, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to let them down, but I’m not really into it. I’m moving the pieces and trying to act normal, but inside I’m just kind of going through the motions. So with friends, yeah, I’ll avoid plans or I’ll show up and not really feel present. It doesn’t bring me much pleasure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I’m not, like, pacing around the room or tapping my foot so much people notice, at least I don’t think I am.\n\nI mean, sometimes I shift around if I’m sitting too long, but I’ve always done some of that. On job sites I’m used to being up and moving, so sitting still in a chair can feel weird after a while, but I wouldn’t say I feel keyed up or like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit there longer than I mean to sometimes.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed fidgeting or being unable to sit still as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly. It’s like everything gets turned back on me somehow.\n\nI keep thinking I’ve failed my wife and my kids. Like I’m supposed to be the steady one, the guy who handles things, and instead I feel like I’m letting them down all the time. Even little things — if I miss an appointment, or forget something I said I’d take care of, it doesn’t just feel like a mistake. It feels like proof that I’m not any good at this, at being a husband or a father.\n\nAnd work is the same way. I manage crews, so there are always things going wrong — costs running over, schedules slipping, some safety issue that needs attention — and I know, logically, some of that is just the job. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head. It feels like if anything goes wrong, it’s because I missed something, or I should’ve been sharper, or I shouldn’t be the one in charge at all. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking, “How did I let that happen?” Even if other people tell me it wasn’t all on me, I don’t really believe them.\n\nIt gets to where I feel useless. Like everyone would be better off if someone else was handling things instead of me. At home, at work… just anywhere I’m supposed to be responsible. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to initiate activities as easily as you used to?\n\nPatient: No… not like I used to. It feels like there’s this extra weight between thinking about doing something and actually doing it.\n\nI can know, in my head, “I should go out to the garage and sort my fishing gear,” or “I could go spend an hour in the woodshop,” but then I just… don’t. The fishing stuff is still sitting there untouched, and that used to be something I’d mess with just because I enjoyed it. Now even getting started feels pointless, like I’m trying to force myself into being interested.\n\nSame with the woodshop. I’ll walk out there sometimes, look around, and instead of feeling that pull to start a project, it just feels like another chore waiting on me. And with my kids, if they ask me to play chess, I’ll do it because they’re my kids and I don’t want to disappoint them, but I’m not the one initiating it. I’m mostly just sitting there going through the motions.\n\nSo, no, I’d say starting things is a lot harder now. It’s not even that the activities are complicated. I just don’t seem to have that spark to begin them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving at a slower pace than usual in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t notice myself walking slower in the way somebody would look at me and say, “What’s wrong with you?” At least nobody’s said that to me.\n\nDay to day, though… I do feel like things take longer, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m physically moving slower or because I’m just slower to get going. Like getting dressed, making coffee, getting out the door — sometimes it feels like I’m dragging through it, but I can’t tell if my body is actually slow or if it’s more that I’m kind of stuck in my head and not moving from one thing to the next.\n\nAt work I can still move around a site when I need to. If there’s something that has to be handled, I can walk over there, talk to whoever, check what needs checking. I don’t think I’m shuffling around or anything like that. But when there isn’t something pushing me, I probably do sort of… stall out. Sit longer. Take more time than I mean to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed that I’m moving slower, exactly. More that everything feels harder to start and takes more effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have been talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mean, I’m probably quieter than I used to be, or I take longer to answer sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as talking more slowly.\n\nNobody at home or work has said, “You’re speaking slower,” or anything like that. Sometimes I do feel like I have to search for the words a little more, or I pause because I’m trying to get my thoughts in order, but I can’t tell if it comes across as slow speech from the outside.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t caught myself speaking slower in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a significant weight loss, like more than 5% of your body weight, over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, or at least it seems like it. I haven’t been carefully tracking the number on the scale or anything, so I don’t want to pretend I can give you an exact percentage. But my belt has had to come in a couple notches, and that wasn’t because I was trying to lose weight or exercising more.\n\nFood just doesn’t do much for me most days. It’s like it tastes flat, almost like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast a lot, not even in a planned way — I just don’t want anything, or I look at it and it feels like too much effort to eat. Then I’ll be out on a job site all day, and you’d think I’d come home hungry, but at dinner I mostly pick at whatever’s there.\n\nSo… significant? I don’t know the exact number, but it’s noticeable enough that my clothes fit different, and it hasn’t been intentional.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing more restless physical activities than usual, like pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the pacing-around kind of way, at least. I’m trying to think because sometimes I’ll get up and move from one room to another without really having a plan, but I wouldn’t say I’m wearing a path in the floor or anything like that.\n\nAt home, I’m probably more likely to just sit there than pace. If I’m waiting for a call back or something at work, I might stand up, check my phone, walk a few steps, look out the window, sit back down — but that feels pretty normal to me, or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed increasing in some obvious way.\n\nNobody’s said I seem restless or like I can’t sit still. If anything, I feel like I’m doing less of that kind of busy movement. So… no, I haven’t really noticed more restless physical activity like pacing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s pretty consistently the same, honestly. I wish I could say there are days where I suddenly feel like myself again, but… not really.

The main thing is I just stopped caring about the stuff I used to look forward to. Like, my fishing gear is still sitting in the garage. I see it there, and part of me knows that normally I’d be checking the rods or organizing tackle or thinking about where to go on a weekend, but now it just sits there. I don’t even really feel guilty enough to do anything about it.

Same with the woodshop. That used to be something I could lose hours in. I’d start messing with a piece of wood and actually enjoy figuring it out. Now when I look at it, it feels like another chore, like something I’m supposed to care about but don’t.

And even with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to hurt their feelings, but I’m just going through the motions. I move the pieces, I try to act engaged, but inside it’s like there’s nothing there. So no, I don’t really have many better days where I’m more interested. It’s mostly just that same level of not caring.

I notice more that I’m not myself, but if you’re asking specifically whether I feel restless or anxious, I’d say… maybe some, but I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I haven’t really been weighing myself every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I can tell from my clothes.\n\nMy belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t do anything to make that happen. I’m not dieting, I’m not trying to get in shape. If anything, with work, I should probably be eating more, especially being out on job sites all day. But most days food just… doesn’t appeal to me. It sounds strange, but it’s like everything tastes like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast without really deciding to, just because nothing sounds worth eating, and then by dinner I’ll sit there and kind of pick at the food.\n\nSometimes I know I should be hungry, because I’ve been moving around or dealing with a long day, but I just don’t want it. I haven’t noticed myself speaking slower, exactly… but I’m probably not the best judge of that. I don’t know if it’s actually slower or if everything just feels like it takes more effort. Like getting up, getting started, doing the next thing — it can feel like I’m dragging myself through it.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice the most, because it’s not just feeling “tired” at the end of a long day, like you’d expect. It’s more like I start the day already behind.

I’ll get up and get myself moving, and by midmorning I feel like I’ve already worked a full shift. And in my job, I’m used to being on my feet, walking the project, going up and down stairs, checking on crews, dealing with problems as they come up. That used to be normal for me. But now even walking the site or climbing stairs with the guys feels like my body is carrying wet concrete.

It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but everything feels heavier than it should. I’ll look at something I need to do and it’s not even that the task is impossible — it’s just like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. I have to push myself through basic parts of the day that I used to just do without thinking. So yes, I have way less energy than I used to.

It sounds strange, but it’s like everything tastes like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast without really deciding to, just because nothing sounds worth eating, and then by dinner I’ll sit there and kind of pick at the food.\n\nSometimes I know I should be hungry, because I’ve been moving around or dealing with a long day, but I just don’t want it. Sometimes I do feel like I have to search for the words a little more, or I pause because I’m trying to get my thoughts in order, but I can’t tell if it comes across as slow speech from the outside.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t caught myself speaking slower in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a significant weight loss, like more than 5% of your body weight, over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, or at least it seems like it. But I haven’t had anyone say, “Why are you moving so slow?” or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed it as moving or talking slower, not in a way I can point to. It’s more that I feel slowed down inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know how much that shows on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends or is that also something that doesn't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… friends too, I guess. It’s not just friends, though. It’s kind of everything I used to actually look forward to.\n\nLike, I used to be the guy who’d be thinking about fishing even when I was at work—checking gear, planning where I might go, messing with the tackle box in the garage. And now all that stuff is just sitting there. I see it when I walk past and I just… don’t care. It’s not that I decided I don’t like fishing anymore. It’s more like the part of me that would get excited about it just isn’t there.\n\nSame with my woodshop. I used to be able to spend hours out there and lose track of time. Now if I go in there, it feels like another task I’m supposed to do, like a chore, not something I enjoy. I’ll look at a project and just feel tired of it before I even start.\n\nAnd with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess sometimes, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to let them down, but I’m not really into it. I’m moving the pieces and trying to act normal, but inside I’m just kind of going through the motions. So with friends, yeah, I’ll avoid plans or I’ll show up and not really feel present. It doesn’t bring me much pleasure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I’m not, like, pacing around the room or tapping my foot so much people notice, at least I don’t think I am.\n\nI mean, sometimes I shift around if I’m sitting too long, but I’ve always done some of that. On job sites I’m used to being up and moving, so sitting still in a chair can feel weird after a while, but I wouldn’t say I feel keyed up or like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit there longer than I mean to sometimes.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed fidgeting or being unable to sit still as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly. It’s like everything gets turned back on me somehow.\n\nI keep thinking I’ve failed my wife and my kids. Like I’m supposed to be the steady one, the guy who handles things, and instead I feel like I’m letting them down all the time. Even little things — if I miss an appointment, or forget something I said I’d take care of, it doesn’t just feel like a mistake. It feels like proof that I’m not any good at this, at being a husband or a father.\n\nAnd work is the same way. I manage crews, so there are always things going wrong — costs running over, schedules slipping, some safety issue that needs attention — and I know, logically, some of that is just the job. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head. It feels like if anything goes wrong, it’s because I missed something, or I should’ve been sharper, or I shouldn’t be the one in charge at all. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking, “How did I let that happen?” Even if other people tell me it wasn’t all on me, I don’t really believe them.\n\nIt gets to where I feel useless. Like everyone would be better off if someone else was handling things instead of me. At home, at work… just anywhere I’m supposed to be responsible. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to initiate activities as easily as you used to?\n\nPatient: No… not like I used to. It feels like there’s this extra weight between thinking about doing something and actually doing it.\n\nI can know, in my head, “I should go out to the garage and sort my fishing gear,” or “I could go spend an hour in the woodshop,” but then I just… don’t. The fishing stuff is still sitting there untouched, and that used to be something I’d mess with just because I enjoyed it. Now even getting started feels pointless, like I’m trying to force myself into being interested.\n\nSame with the woodshop. I’ll walk out there sometimes, look around, and instead of feeling that pull to start a project, it just feels like another chore waiting on me. And with my kids, if they ask me to play chess, I’ll do it because they’re my kids and I don’t want to disappoint them, but I’m not the one initiating it. I’m mostly just sitting there going through the motions.\n\nSo, no, I’d say starting things is a lot harder now. It’s not even that the activities are complicated. I just don’t seem to have that spark to begin them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving at a slower pace than usual in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t notice myself walking slower in the way somebody would look at me and say, “What’s wrong with you?” At least nobody’s said that to me.\n\nDay to day, though… I do feel like things take longer, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m physically moving slower or because I’m just slower to get going. Like getting dressed, making coffee, getting out the door — sometimes it feels like I’m dragging through it, but I can’t tell if my body is actually slow or if it’s more that I’m kind of stuck in my head and not moving from one thing to the next.\n\nAt work I can still move around a site when I need to. If there’s something that has to be handled, I can walk over there, talk to whoever, check what needs checking. I don’t think I’m shuffling around or anything like that. But when there isn’t something pushing me, I probably do sort of… stall out. Sit longer. Take more time than I mean to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed that I’m moving slower, exactly. More that everything feels harder to start and takes more effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have been talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mean, I’m probably quieter than I used to be, or I take longer to answer sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as talking more slowly.\n\nNobody at home or work has said, “You’re speaking slower,” or anything like that. Sometimes I do feel like I have to search for the words a little more, or I pause because I’m trying to get my thoughts in order, but I can’t tell if it comes across as slow speech from the outside.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t caught myself speaking slower in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a significant weight loss, like more than 5% of your body weight, over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, or at least it seems like it. I haven’t been carefully tracking the number on the scale or anything, so I don’t want to pretend I can give you an exact percentage. But my belt has had to come in a couple notches, and that wasn’t because I was trying to lose weight or exercising more.\n\nFood just doesn’t do much for me most days. It’s like it tastes flat, almost like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast a lot, not even in a planned way — I just don’t want anything, or I look at it and it feels like too much effort to eat. Then I’ll be out on a job site all day, and you’d think I’d come home hungry, but at dinner I mostly pick at whatever’s there.\n\nSo… significant? I don’t know the exact number, but it’s noticeable enough that my clothes fit different, and it hasn’t been intentional.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing more restless physical activities than usual, like pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the pacing-around kind of way, at least. I’m trying to think because sometimes I’ll get up and move from one room to another without really having a plan, but I wouldn’t say I’m wearing a path in the floor or anything like that.\n\nAt home, I’m probably more likely to just sit there than pace. If I’m waiting for a call back or something at work, I might stand up, check my phone, walk a few steps, look out the window, sit back down — but that feels pretty normal to me, or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed increasing in some obvious way.\n\nNobody’s said I seem restless or like I can’t sit still. If anything, I feel like I’m doing less of that kind of busy movement. So… no, I haven’t really noticed more restless physical activity like pacing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel more interested in things, or is it consistently the same level of not caring?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistently the same, honestly. I wish I could say there are days where I suddenly feel like myself again, but… not really.\n\nThe main thing is I just stopped caring about the stuff I used to look forward to. Like, my fishing gear is still sitting in the garage. I see it there, and part of me knows that normally I’d be checking the rods or organizing tackle or thinking about where to go on a weekend, but now it just sits there. I don’t even really feel guilty enough to do anything about it.\n\nSame with the woodshop. That used to be something I could lose hours in. I’d start messing with a piece of wood and actually enjoy figuring it out. Now when I look at it, it feels like another chore, like something I’m supposed to care about but don’t.\n\nAnd even with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to hurt their feelings, but I’m just going through the motions. I move the pieces, I try to act engaged, but inside it’s like there’s nothing there. So no, I don’t really have many better days where I’m more interested. It’s mostly just that same level of not caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy to do things than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice the most, because it’s not just feeling “tired” at the end of a long day, like you’d expect. It’s more like I start the day already behind.\n\nI’ll get up and get myself moving, and by midmorning I feel like I’ve already worked a full shift. And in my job, I’m used to being on my feet, walking the project, going up and down stairs, checking on crews, dealing with problems as they come up. That used to be normal for me. But now even walking the site or climbing stairs with the guys feels like my body is carrying wet concrete.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but everything feels heavier than it should. I’ll look at something I need to do and it’s not even that the task is impossible — it’s just like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. I have to push myself through basic parts of the day that I used to just do without thinking. So yes, I have way less energy than I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t think so, no — at least not in a way that I’d call agitated behavior. I mean, I can get uncomfortable in my own skin sometimes, but if you mean things like pacing around, fidgeting constantly, snapping into activity, or seeming like I can’t sit still, I haven’t really noticed that.

If anything, when I’m not doing what I have to do, I’m more likely to just sit there and kind of… stay put. There are little things, sure — I might rub my face, shift around in a chair, check my phone without really needing to — but that doesn’t feel like some big change from before. And I don’t think my wife or anyone at work has pointed out that I’m acting keyed up or restless.

So I guess my answer is no, not more than usual. If you’re asking about feeling unsettled inside, maybe sometimes, but outwardly I don’t think I’m doing more restless or agitated stuff.

It’s that even when I do the things, I don’t feel much from them anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. About a year and seven months, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt really started after my wife’s health problems changed everything at home. I don’t think it happened all at once, like one day I woke up and everything was flat. It was more like things kept shifting, and I kept telling myself I just had to get through the next week, then the next thing, then the next appointment or adjustment at home… and somewhere in there I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nAt first I thought it was just stress, or that I was worn out from everything changing. But it’s been over a year and a half now, and it hasn’t really lifted. That’s part of what scares me, honestly — that it’s gone on this long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there making some detailed plan all day or anything, but there are these moments that scare me. Usually it’s early in the morning, when I’m driving before anyone else is really awake, and everything’s quiet. I’ll be on the road and this thought just comes in like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t make it where I’m going.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further, where I catch myself thinking about steering off the road. That’s the part that really scares me, because it doesn’t feel as distant as I want it to. It feels too close, like it’s not just some random dark thought I can brush away. I don’t want to talk about it, but I guess I’m here because it’s been happening and it’s frightening me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of restlessness, nervousness, or anxiety lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m pacing around all the time or anything like that, at least not that I’ve really noticed. Nervousness… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if I’d call it anxiety or just being wound up from everything. It’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. A lot of the time I just feel heavy and worn down, and then other times there’s this tense feeling in the background, like I can’t quite settle, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t been paying attention to it as its own thing. I notice more that I’m not myself, but if you’re asking specifically whether I feel restless or anxious, I’d say… maybe some, but I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I haven’t really been weighing myself every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I can tell from my clothes.\n\nMy belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t do anything to make that happen. I’m not dieting, I’m not trying to get in shape. If anything, with work, I should probably be eating more, especially being out on job sites all day. But most days food just… doesn’t appeal to me. It sounds strange, but it’s like everything tastes like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast without really deciding to, just because nothing sounds worth eating, and then by dinner I’ll sit there and kind of pick at the food.\n\nSometimes I know I should be hungry, because I’ve been moving around or dealing with a long day, but I just don’t want it. So, yeah… I guess I have lost weight, even if I don’t know the exact amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself speaking slower, exactly… but I’m probably not the best judge of that. I mean, I know sometimes I take longer to answer people, but that might just be because I’m trying to think of what to say or I don’t have much to say.\n\nWith moving slower… maybe? I don’t know if it’s actually slower or if everything just feels like it takes more effort. Like getting up, getting started, doing the next thing — it can feel like I’m dragging myself through it. But I haven’t had anyone say, “Why are you moving so slow?” or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed it as moving or talking slower, not in a way I can point to. It’s more that I feel slowed down inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know how much that shows on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends or is that also something that doesn't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… friends too, I guess. It’s not just friends, though. It’s kind of everything I used to actually look forward to.\n\nLike, I used to be the guy who’d be thinking about fishing even when I was at work—checking gear, planning where I might go, messing with the tackle box in the garage. And now all that stuff is just sitting there. I see it when I walk past and I just… don’t care. It’s not that I decided I don’t like fishing anymore. It’s more like the part of me that would get excited about it just isn’t there.\n\nSame with my woodshop. I used to be able to spend hours out there and lose track of time. Now if I go in there, it feels like another task I’m supposed to do, like a chore, not something I enjoy. I’ll look at a project and just feel tired of it before I even start.\n\nAnd with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess sometimes, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to let them down, but I’m not really into it. I’m moving the pieces and trying to act normal, but inside I’m just kind of going through the motions. So with friends, yeah, I’ll avoid plans or I’ll show up and not really feel present. It doesn’t bring me much pleasure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I’m not, like, pacing around the room or tapping my foot so much people notice, at least I don’t think I am.\n\nI mean, sometimes I shift around if I’m sitting too long, but I’ve always done some of that. On job sites I’m used to being up and moving, so sitting still in a chair can feel weird after a while, but I wouldn’t say I feel keyed up or like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit there longer than I mean to sometimes.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed fidgeting or being unable to sit still as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly. It’s like everything gets turned back on me somehow.\n\nI keep thinking I’ve failed my wife and my kids. Like I’m supposed to be the steady one, the guy who handles things, and instead I feel like I’m letting them down all the time. Even little things — if I miss an appointment, or forget something I said I’d take care of, it doesn’t just feel like a mistake. It feels like proof that I’m not any good at this, at being a husband or a father.\n\nAnd work is the same way. I manage crews, so there are always things going wrong — costs running over, schedules slipping, some safety issue that needs attention — and I know, logically, some of that is just the job. But it doesn’t feel that way in my head. It feels like if anything goes wrong, it’s because I missed something, or I should’ve been sharper, or I shouldn’t be the one in charge at all. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking, “How did I let that happen?” Even if other people tell me it wasn’t all on me, I don’t really believe them.\n\nIt gets to where I feel useless. Like everyone would be better off if someone else was handling things instead of me. At home, at work… just anywhere I’m supposed to be responsible. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to initiate activities as easily as you used to?\n\nPatient: No… not like I used to. It feels like there’s this extra weight between thinking about doing something and actually doing it.\n\nI can know, in my head, “I should go out to the garage and sort my fishing gear,” or “I could go spend an hour in the woodshop,” but then I just… don’t. The fishing stuff is still sitting there untouched, and that used to be something I’d mess with just because I enjoyed it. Now even getting started feels pointless, like I’m trying to force myself into being interested.\n\nSame with the woodshop. I’ll walk out there sometimes, look around, and instead of feeling that pull to start a project, it just feels like another chore waiting on me. And with my kids, if they ask me to play chess, I’ll do it because they’re my kids and I don’t want to disappoint them, but I’m not the one initiating it. I’m mostly just sitting there going through the motions.\n\nSo, no, I’d say starting things is a lot harder now. It’s not even that the activities are complicated. I just don’t seem to have that spark to begin them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving at a slower pace than usual in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t notice myself walking slower in the way somebody would look at me and say, “What’s wrong with you?” At least nobody’s said that to me.\n\nDay to day, though… I do feel like things take longer, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m physically moving slower or because I’m just slower to get going. Like getting dressed, making coffee, getting out the door — sometimes it feels like I’m dragging through it, but I can’t tell if my body is actually slow or if it’s more that I’m kind of stuck in my head and not moving from one thing to the next.\n\nAt work I can still move around a site when I need to. If there’s something that has to be handled, I can walk over there, talk to whoever, check what needs checking. I don’t think I’m shuffling around or anything like that. But when there isn’t something pushing me, I probably do sort of… stall out. Sit longer. Take more time than I mean to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed that I’m moving slower, exactly. More that everything feels harder to start and takes more effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have been talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mean, I’m probably quieter than I used to be, or I take longer to answer sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as talking more slowly.\n\nNobody at home or work has said, “You’re speaking slower,” or anything like that. Sometimes I do feel like I have to search for the words a little more, or I pause because I’m trying to get my thoughts in order, but I can’t tell if it comes across as slow speech from the outside.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t caught myself speaking slower in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a significant weight loss, like more than 5% of your body weight, over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, or at least it seems like it. I haven’t been carefully tracking the number on the scale or anything, so I don’t want to pretend I can give you an exact percentage. But my belt has had to come in a couple notches, and that wasn’t because I was trying to lose weight or exercising more.\n\nFood just doesn’t do much for me most days. It’s like it tastes flat, almost like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast a lot, not even in a planned way — I just don’t want anything, or I look at it and it feels like too much effort to eat. Then I’ll be out on a job site all day, and you’d think I’d come home hungry, but at dinner I mostly pick at whatever’s there.\n\nSo… significant? I don’t know the exact number, but it’s noticeable enough that my clothes fit different, and it hasn’t been intentional.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing more restless physical activities than usual, like pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the pacing-around kind of way, at least. I’m trying to think because sometimes I’ll get up and move from one room to another without really having a plan, but I wouldn’t say I’m wearing a path in the floor or anything like that.\n\nAt home, I’m probably more likely to just sit there than pace. If I’m waiting for a call back or something at work, I might stand up, check my phone, walk a few steps, look out the window, sit back down — but that feels pretty normal to me, or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed increasing in some obvious way.\n\nNobody’s said I seem restless or like I can’t sit still. If anything, I feel like I’m doing less of that kind of busy movement. So… no, I haven’t really noticed more restless physical activity like pacing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel more interested in things, or is it consistently the same level of not caring?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistently the same, honestly. I wish I could say there are days where I suddenly feel like myself again, but… not really.\n\nThe main thing is I just stopped caring about the stuff I used to look forward to. Like, my fishing gear is still sitting in the garage. I see it there, and part of me knows that normally I’d be checking the rods or organizing tackle or thinking about where to go on a weekend, but now it just sits there. I don’t even really feel guilty enough to do anything about it.\n\nSame with the woodshop. That used to be something I could lose hours in. I’d start messing with a piece of wood and actually enjoy figuring it out. Now when I look at it, it feels like another chore, like something I’m supposed to care about but don’t.\n\nAnd even with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to hurt their feelings, but I’m just going through the motions. I move the pieces, I try to act engaged, but inside it’s like there’s nothing there. So no, I don’t really have many better days where I’m more interested. It’s mostly just that same level of not caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy to do things than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice the most, because it’s not just feeling “tired” at the end of a long day, like you’d expect. It’s more like I start the day already behind.\n\nI’ll get up and get myself moving, and by midmorning I feel like I’ve already worked a full shift. And in my job, I’m used to being on my feet, walking the project, going up and down stairs, checking on crews, dealing with problems as they come up. That used to be normal for me. But now even walking the site or climbing stairs with the guys feels like my body is carrying wet concrete.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but everything feels heavier than it should. I’ll look at something I need to do and it’s not even that the task is impossible — it’s just like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. I have to push myself through basic parts of the day that I used to just do without thinking. So yes, I have way less energy than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself engaging in more restless or agitated behavior than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — at least not in a way that I’d call agitated behavior. I mean, I can get uncomfortable in my own skin sometimes, but if you mean things like pacing around, fidgeting constantly, snapping into activity, or seeming like I can’t sit still, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nIf anything, when I’m not doing what I have to do, I’m more likely to just sit there and kind of… stay put. There are little things, sure — I might rub my face, shift around in a chair, check my phone without really needing to — but that doesn’t feel like some big change from before. And I don’t think my wife or anyone at work has pointed out that I’m acting keyed up or restless.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not more than usual. If you’re asking about feeling unsettled inside, maybe sometimes, but outwardly I don’t think I’m doing more restless or agitated stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Sometimes I do feel like I have to search for the words a little more, or I pause because I’m trying to get my thoughts in order, but I can’t tell if it comes across as slow speech from the outside.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t caught myself speaking slower in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a significant weight loss, like more than 5% of your body weight, over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, or at least it seems like it. I haven’t been carefully tracking the number on the scale or anything, so I don’t want to pretend I can give you an exact percentage. But my belt has had to come in a couple notches, and that wasn’t because I was trying to lose weight or exercising more.\n\nFood just doesn’t do much for me most days. It’s like it tastes flat, almost like cardboard. I’ll skip breakfast a lot, not even in a planned way — I just don’t want anything, or I look at it and it feels like too much effort to eat. Then I’ll be out on a job site all day, and you’d think I’d come home hungry, but at dinner I mostly pick at whatever’s there.\n\nSo… significant? I don’t know the exact number, but it’s noticeable enough that my clothes fit different, and it hasn’t been intentional.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing more restless physical activities than usual, like pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the pacing-around kind of way, at least. I’m trying to think because sometimes I’ll get up and move from one room to another without really having a plan, but I wouldn’t say I’m wearing a path in the floor or anything like that.\n\nAt home, I’m probably more likely to just sit there than pace. If I’m waiting for a call back or something at work, I might stand up, check my phone, walk a few steps, look out the window, sit back down — but that feels pretty normal to me, or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed increasing in some obvious way.\n\nNobody’s said I seem restless or like I can’t sit still. If anything, I feel like I’m doing less of that kind of busy movement. So… no, I haven’t really noticed more restless physical activity like pacing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel more interested in things, or is it consistently the same level of not caring?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistently the same, honestly. I wish I could say there are days where I suddenly feel like myself again, but… not really.\n\nThe main thing is I just stopped caring about the stuff I used to look forward to. Like, my fishing gear is still sitting in the garage. I see it there, and part of me knows that normally I’d be checking the rods or organizing tackle or thinking about where to go on a weekend, but now it just sits there. I don’t even really feel guilty enough to do anything about it.\n\nSame with the woodshop. That used to be something I could lose hours in. I’d start messing with a piece of wood and actually enjoy figuring it out. Now when I look at it, it feels like another chore, like something I’m supposed to care about but don’t.\n\nAnd even with my kids… they’ll ask me to play chess, and I’ll sit down because I don’t want to hurt their feelings, but I’m just going through the motions. I move the pieces, I try to act engaged, but inside it’s like there’s nothing there. So no, I don’t really have many better days where I’m more interested. It’s mostly just that same level of not caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy to do things than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice the most, because it’s not just feeling “tired” at the end of a long day, like you’d expect. It’s more like I start the day already behind.\n\nI’ll get up and get myself moving, and by midmorning I feel like I’ve already worked a full shift. And in my job, I’m used to being on my feet, walking the project, going up and down stairs, checking on crews, dealing with problems as they come up. That used to be normal for me. But now even walking the site or climbing stairs with the guys feels like my body is carrying wet concrete.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but everything feels heavier than it should. I’ll look at something I need to do and it’s not even that the task is impossible — it’s just like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. I have to push myself through basic parts of the day that I used to just do without thinking. So yes, I have way less energy than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself engaging in more restless or agitated behavior than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — at least not in a way that I’d call agitated behavior. I mean, I can get uncomfortable in my own skin sometimes, but if you mean things like pacing around, fidgeting constantly, snapping into activity, or seeming like I can’t sit still, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nIf anything, when I’m not doing what I have to do, I’m more likely to just sit there and kind of… stay put. There are little things, sure — I might rub my face, shift around in a chair, check my phone without really needing to — but that doesn’t feel like some big change from before. And I don’t think my wife or anyone at work has pointed out that I’m acting keyed up or restless.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not more than usual. I don’t think it happened all at once, like one day I woke up and everything was flat. It was more like things kept shifting, and I kept telling myself I just had to get through the next week, then the next thing, then the next appointment or adjustment at home… and somewhere in there I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nAt first I thought it was just stress, or that I was worn out from everything changing. But it’s been over a year and a half now, and it hasn’t really lifted. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there making some detailed plan all day or anything, but there are these moments that scare me. I haven’t really been weighing myself every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I can tell from my clothes.\n\nMy belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t do anything to make that happen.
